Nothing on the page has him deciding to carry the physical registers box east after that; the established logic runs the other way — he got what he needed from them at the bench and gave up on reading them further. 3. **"And you'll be gone longer than two weeks," said Sorrel... "You've told me two weeks four times today and the rider once."** — On the page for Day 6 (scenes 19–22), Edwin says "two weeks" to Sorrel only twice, both in the same speech in scene 21 ("Two weeks... No instance of him saying "two weeks" to the Wystow rider appears on the page (his two letters, described in scene 21, don't state a duration, and his exchange with the rider in scene 22 is about the letters, not duration). So the count of times told *to her* is 2, not 4, and "the rider once" is uncontradicted but uncited. Minimal replacement: change to a count consistent with the visible page — "You've told me two weeks twice today, and the girl once" — preserving the reason (that he's repeated it enough to be worth doubting).<br>
<br>

&lt;result&gt;## Findings — scene 23, "The good staff"<br>
<br>
**Part 1**<br>
<br>
1. **"You've not said what you're going to look at, all day," she said. "Not to me, not to the girl."** — Part 1, *check every count and every fact* / *a line that contradicts itself*. He told Sorrel the whole of it that same morning, in detail (scene 19: the grass spending its savings, the rings, Fenholt, the chain), and she then recites that same content back at him here, which contradicts the charge she has just made. Replace with the true version — that he said it once and has been calling it a consulting trip ever since: *"You told me the whole of it this morning and you've not said it since," she said. "Not to the girl at all. Since noon it's been a field, and a crop in rings, and a consulting trip, and two weeks. So here it is."*<br>
<br>
2. **"You've told me two weeks four times today and the rider once."** — Part 1, *count the occurrences*. On the page he says two weeks to Sorrel in one speech (scene 21) and to Peri (scene 22); what he said to the rider was that there would be two letters, and nothing about a fortnight. Replace: *"You've told me two weeks twice today and the girl once."*<br>
<br>
3. **"He took one out again, because Fenholt had washing water like anywhere else and two weeks was two weeks."** — Part 1, *a reason attached to nothing / the clauses disagree*. The first clause argues for fewer robes, the second argues for more, and they are joined as if they agreed. Replace the second clause so both pull one way: *"because Fenholt had washing water like anywhere else, and a farmer's household washes for a guest."*<br>
<br>
4. **"If you take three you'll be carrying a wet one down a road, because you'll not wait for it to dry before you leave, because you never have."** — Part 1, *does the justification distinguish?* The wet robe follows from the household washing one and him leaving early, which happens whether he packs two or three; her reason does not separate two from three. Replace: *"If you take three, the third comes home dry and unworn, and you'll have carried it eighty miles to prove you didn't need it."*<br>
<br>
5. **"Most days he had no need of the breath. On those days it had been the whole of the difference…"** — Part 1, *a pronoun with no clear antecedent*. "Those days" grammatically points at the most-days just named, and means the opposite. Replace: *"Most days he had no need of the breath. On the days in that list it was the whole of the difference, and he had never once worked one of them without the staff."*<br>
<br>
6. **"You've a use for all three. You've just told yourself what they are."** — Part 1, *check the facts inside the scene*. He weighed them in silence; the uses were narration, not speech, so she is crediting him with something the room did not contain. Replace: *"You've a use for all three. You weighed them and you put them down in the order you use them."*<br>
<br>
7. **She went to the corner … "It isn't," said Sorrel** — Part 1, *check the dependents*, against scene 19. *The good staff* was the fourth item on the list she wrote in front of him that morning, and he read it over her shoulder without objecting; the scene plays the staff as a thing that has not yet come up. Do not cut the beat — own it: add, before she crosses the room, *It had been the last thing on the list she wrote that morning, and he had read it over her shoulder and said nothing about it then either.*<br>
<br>
8. **"She picked the frost list up off the desk"** — Part 1, *the wrong noun for a real thing*. The line she then speaks is "Fourth item on my list", about a gate latch; the frost list is a different document and has nothing to do with it. Replace: *"She picked her own list up off the desk, where it had been all evening."*<br>
<br>
9. **"The gate at the third bend," said Edwin.** — Part 1, *a reply that does not answer the line before it*. It follows her flat statement that he will be gone longer than a fortnight, and the swerve is deliberate but the narration does not own it, so it reads as a break in the scene rather than as him ducking. Add one clause: *Edwin let that stand where it was. "The gate at the third bend," he said.*<br>
<br>
10. **"because he was going to that field to be believed about it"** — Part 1, *a word doing a job it cannot do*. "Believed about it" will not carry the meaning; the antecedent of "it" is the field. Replace: *"because he was going to that field to be believed, and a man in the robe he has walked two days in is believed less."*<br>
<br>
11. **"which was the one thing about carrying a pack that he was certain of"** — Part 1, *does it distinguish?* He was certain about six other things that afternoon (twelve miles, boots, stockings, bread that keeps, not cheese, a knife he can sharpen). Replace: *"which was the thing about carrying a pack he was surest of."*<br>
<br>
**Part 4**<br>
<br>
12. **"I've told her, and her neighbours, and the whole of that parish by supper"** — Part 4 / edwin.md 7a, the inventory pile of named instances is <span class="redacted">redacted: character</span>'s construction and banned for him. Replace: *"I've told that parish by supper, and her with it."*<br>
<br>
13. **"That's my reason. Yours is that you'd rather not be seen carrying it. Mine's the better one."** — Part 4, *what does this person want and what do they know*, plus a construction leak: "That's your reason. It's the better one here" is Edwin's marked concession in canon (edwin.md 2), and her paraphrase also misstates his reason, which was what the farmer and her neighbours will conclude, not vanity. Replace: *"That's my reason. Yours is what her neighbours make of a staff. One of us is going to be wrong about something tomorrow and I'd rather it was you about the neighbours."*<br>
<br>
**Part 2 / Part 5**<br>
<br>
14. **"the whole of the subject" … "the whole of the difference" … "the whole of that parish"** — Part 5, *a construction used more than once in a scene is that scene's tic*. Three in 1,550 words, and the first two are the empty kind. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
<br>
15. **"He weighed them anyway, one at a time, and laid them side by side, and looked at them."** — Part 6, *prefer the participial form to a chain of "and"s in a physical beat*. Replace: *"He weighed them anyway, one at a time, laid them side by side, and looked at them."*<br>
<br>
Em-dash count is 1 in 1,549 words, well inside budget; no "[amount] of the", "I'll say it", "to be mine" or "it's against you"; one narrator aphorism (the kit of a man who had not been anywhere in forty years); Sorrel spends "Hm" once and carries a list-ordinal; Edwin's bare "No." is attached to its reason.<br>
<br>

<br>
## Part 1 — does this sentence make sense<br>
<br>
**"Everything that could be handed over had been handed over the night before last"**<br>
Part 1, *check every count and every fact inside the scene*. <span class="redacted">redacted: theme</span> was **last night**: scenes 20–23 are all Day 6 (four o'clock, supper, the two lists, the Drovers at ten, packing at eleven), and this is Day 7's dawn. "The night before last" is Day 5, when nothing had started.<br>
→ "had been handed over the night before, entry by entry, in three hours at the kitchen table, and gone through twice since"<br>
<br>
**"Mrs Hobbet was in the yard too, and had been up that hill before either of them."**<br>
Part 1, *check every count and fact*. Edwin did not come up the hill; he came down out of his own tower. The claim is only true of Peri.<br>
→ "Mrs Hobbet was in the yard too, and had come up the hill in the dark, before Peri did."<br>
<br>
**"the one she gave Peri had a P worked in thread on the knot, because she had asked at the inn what the girl wouldn't eat, and the answer had been onion, and there was no onion in it."**<br>
Part 1, *viewpoint knowledge*. Edwin cannot know what is inside a wrapped breakfast, and he was not at the inn for the asking. The fact is good and canon; it needs a mouth.<br>
→ Give it to Mrs Hobbet: "The one with the P is yours," she said to Peri. "There's no onion in it. I asked at the inn what you wouldn't eat." Then Edwin need only see the P.<br>
<br>
**"and I think he meant it as thanks."**<br>
Part 1, *a reply/gloss that does not parse*, and the author's standing comment on the earlier "and he said it happily". Saying the landlady fed the horse better than she fed him is a complaint on its face; "thanks" does not follow from it, and he had three suppers.<br>
→ "and he wasn't complaining. He'd sooner they fed the horse."  (Or the author's own suggested shape: "and it sounded like a compliment.")<br>
<br>
**"the climb had not cost her what it had cost her six days ago: the boots were broken in and honestly dirty, the pack sat low and tight ... and at some point in the week the bright brass pin on her cloak had been traded for a plain one."**<br>
Part 1, *a justification that does not distinguish / a colon that promises evidence and delivers something else*. Boots and pack explain a cheaper climb; the cloak pin does not, and it drifts the sentence from the climb to a general inventory of the week.<br>
→ Keep the colon on the two that bear on the climb and give the pin its own sentence: "...the pack sat low and tight against her back where the first one had ridden up round her ears. At some point in the week the bright brass pin on her cloak had been traded for a plain one."<br>
<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
Part 1, *a cause left out* — the reader is told a fact and not what it means, and the whole exit turns on it.<br>
→ "...and were going to be in full sun an hour earlier than anybody had planned, which would dry them out before the morning was over."<br>
<br>
**"Go on. Three miles of orchard, then barley all the way to the bridge, and you want the barley done before noon."**<br>
VOICE §2, *a character who has a reason gives it*. The deadline has no reason on the page, and Sorrel's whole construction is instruction-*plus*-deadline-plus-reason (as in her preceding "If I wait for the other letter I could be waiting past the frosts", which works).<br>
→ "...and you want the barley done before noon, because there's not a yard of shade on it and this high has one hot day left in it."<br>
<br>
**"because he had left it where a man could not get out of the study without picking it up"**<br>
Part 1, *pronoun with a competing antecedent*. Both the pack and the staff were left by the study door in scene 23; "it" wants to be the staff.<br>
→ "because he had left the staff where a man could not get out of the study without picking it up".<br>
<br>
**"the girl had mended her gate hinge, the Corrys' winch, and half the parish's small jobs in six days"**<br>
Part 1, *a fact overstated into a falsehood* / scale against canon. Canon has the gate hinge, the winch and four hours in the goat shed. "Half the parish's small jobs" is the narrator inflating a real list.<br>
→ "the girl had mended her gate hinge, mucked her goat shed, and put the Corrys' winch back in service, in six days".<br>
<br>
**"Dob nodded once and did not ask how to spell it."**<br>
Part 1, *an inference that does not follow*, and Part 3, *literacy*. Nothing makes Dob a bad speller, "east" is not a hard word, and canon has him literate and writing things up at home — so the joke's premise is invented for the joke.<br>
→ "Dob nodded once. He did not write it down; the writing happened at home, on the wall, when he got there."<br>
<br>
**"gave one to each of them by hand"**<br>
Part 1, *a word doing a job it cannot do* — "by hand" distinguishes nothing here.<br>
→ "and put one into each of their hands".<br>
<br>
## Part 2 — the gesture at meaning<br>
<br>
**"the climb was an opinion, and everybody present could read it."**<br>
Part 2, *a sentence shaped like weight*, plus the footnote clause telling the reader it means something. Say what she meant by coming.<br>
→ "At her age, with that shoulder, at that hour, the climb had cost her a good deal, and she had spent it on purpose: she had come to see the girl off, and everybody in the yard knew it."<br>
<br>
**"East he had."**<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
→ cut.<br>
<br>
**"and at the man carrying both."**<br>
Part 2, and viewpoint: Edwin narrating himself as "the man" to make the look significant.<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
<br>
## Part 3 — would this exist<br>
<br>
**"The wall at home took a thing only when it had finished happening"**<br>
VOICE §2, *understandable to a reader who has not read the previous scene* — "the wall at home" is a callback carrying its whole meaning unexplained, which is the act-two failure in miniature.<br>
→ "Dob kept a wall at his father's cart shed, in chalk, under REMARKABLE THINGS, and the wall took a thing only when it had finished happening; a leaving was not finished until somebody had seen it leave, so he had come up in the dark to see it."<br>
<br>
## Part 6 — weak where it should be strong<br>
<br>
**"'The pack's ready,' said Edwin"**<br>
Part 6 / VOICE §2, *a question answered with a fragment that gestures at one*. It is decodable, but it is the fourth non-answer in a row (Mrs Hobbet's unanswered "You'll look after her", Dob's "which way", Sorrel's turn to the trays), and it is the closing line — exactly the position the author already called awkward once. A scene may end on a plain answer.<br>
→ "'Not particularly,' said Edwin, and started down the first bend, and she fell in beside him."<br>
<br>
**"She said: "**<br>
Part 6, punctuation. The colon before dialogue is not the book's form anywhere else.<br>
→ "She said, 'The rider was saddling...'"<br>
<br>
**Aphorism count.** Three narrator generalisations: "the climb was an opinion", the wall-and-leaving rule, and "she had never once, in eleven years, needed to say anything at a door that the work had not already said." The last is the good one and is canon-load-bearing; the first is fixed above, which brings the scene to one.<br>
<br>
---<br>
<br>
**The device a second round would be about:** the engine — nobody says the thing a parting is for — is currently executed as four consecutive people not answering the question in front of them, which is the shape the act-two verdict named ("people talk cryptically at each other"). It reads as a house style rather than as four different reticences.<br>
<br>
**What it would take:** let exactly one of them say the plain thing straight — Mrs Hobbet's "You'll look after her" is the natural place, since she is the only one in the yard with no eleven-year shorthand to hide in, and Edwin can answer her plainly ("I will") without the scene losing Sorrel's silence, which is the one that matters.&lt;/result&gt;<br>

&lt;/task-notification&gt;</p></blockquote>

&lt;result&gt;## Findings — scene 31, "The pantry door"<br>
<br>
### Part 1 — does this sentence make sense?<br>
<br>
**"and watched some of the fortnight go out of Mattie Ordish's shoulders when he did."** — Part 1, pronoun/reference with no antecedent (and viewpoint knowledge). "The fortnight" is not established until line 41, twenty lines later, and Edwin has not yet been told about it; in paragraph 1 it refers to nothing the reader or the viewpoint has. It also pre-empts the later line, which is the scene's best inference. **Replace:** "and watched her shoulders come down a little when he did."<br>
<br>
**"served by a woman of about fifty with the reddened hands of somebody who works in water"** — Part 1, the wrong noun for a real thing / reference. She was named and met on the page one scene earlier; introducing her as "a woman of about fifty" and only naming her three clauses later reads as a fresh introduction of a stranger. **Replace:** "served by Mattie Ordish, whose hands were the reddened hands of somebody who works in water, and eaten under her eye to see that it was eaten." (This also fixes "under her watching", which is a verbal noun doing an adjective's job.)<br>
<br>
**"The pantry door, on its inside face, carried the whole of it."** — Part 1 (reference: the whole of *what*?) and Part 2 (banned family: "'the whole of it' continues to be a weird recurring phrase that you should avoid"). **Replace:** "The pantry door, on its inside face, carried the record."<br>
<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> **Replace:** "and that was all she said about the fortnight."<br>
<br>
**"Between the chain figures sat the other kind of entry: … the day the hay went in; … the day the sheep came off."** — Part 1, check every count and every fact. The hay went in in Weedmonth (the month of the *second* chain figure) and the sheep came off in Milkmonth, and the loft has held nine weeks, which puts today well past Weedmonth; the sheep entry is not between the two chain figures. **Replace:** "In among them sat the other kind of entry:".<br>
<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> The door shows two chain figures (late Seedmonth, Weedmonth) with nine weeks since; monthly is what he is about to institute, not what she has done. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> **Replace:** "It's a record, and it's the only one I know of, from its first season, and it's dated."<br>
<br>
**"and I'd like you to put your mark against my copy"** — Part 3, literacy. This world writes everything down and she wrote the door; a mark is what someone who cannot write leaves. **Replace:** "and I'd like your name against my copy".<br>
<br>
**"the gate and the thorn stump and the water trough all marked"** vs **"The gatepost and the stump."** — Part 1, the wrong noun. Canon's line runs SW **gatepost** to thorn stump. **Replace:** "the gatepost and the thorn stump and the water trough all marked".<br>
<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> **Replace:** "the same rotation twenty years of her own, and her father's before that."<br>
<br>
**"And the hay off the ring came in green in Weedmonth," said Edwin, reading the entry** — Part 1, viewpoint knowledge: the entry as described says only "the day the hay went in". *Green* is not on the door. **Fix at source:** make the door entry read "the day the hay went in green".<br>
<br>
**"four rough arcs" / "inside the arcs" / "the hay off the ring"** — Part 1, wrong noun for a real thing; the scene uses two names for the same feature and never joins them, and *ring* arrives cold. **Add to the diagram sentence:** "…and four rough arcs across the middle of it, with *the rings* written against them in her hand."<br>
<br>
**"For the people listening who aren't farmers," said Edwin** — Part 1, reference with no antecedent, and Part 4, a line the character could not say: the people in the pantry are Mattie, who is a farmer, and Peri. It reads as an address to the reader. **Replace:** "'You'll want this in the book,' said Edwin, without turning round, because Peri's pencil had stopped,".<br>
<br>
**"and Mattie looked at it longer than four words needed."** — Part 1, count. *sold, Milkmonth* is two words. **Replace:** "longer than two words needed."<br>
<br>
**"Mattie looked at the door, and at his book, and wiped her hands down her apron"** — Part 1, check who is holding what: she picked the lamp back up four lines earlier and does not put it down again. **Replace:** "Mattie looked at the door, and at his book, and set the lamp on the shelf and wiped her hands down her apron, although they were dry."<br>
<br>
**"asked her one question, the only one she asked all evening"** — Part 1 / Part 5, redundancy: the two halves say the same thing. **Replace:** "asked her the only question she asked all evening:".<br>
<br>
**"You'd have asked, so I'm telling you first."** (the replacement under review) — Part 1, a word doing a job it cannot do: the conditional perfect says she is telling him first *instead of* his asking, when what she means is that he was going to ask next. It reads stiff rather than pre-empting. **Replace:** "You were going to ask. So I'm saying it first."<br>
<br>
**"The kettle had not yet boiled when the first knock came at the door, and Mattie set out the good cups without looking to see who it was."** — Part 1, contradicts the dependent line in 32 ("the good cups were out before the knocking started"), and "the door" is ambiguous in a scene about a door. **Replace:** "The kettle had not yet boiled when Mattie took the good cups down off the dresser and set them out on the table, and nobody had knocked yet."<br>
<br>
### Part 2 — the gesture at meaning<br>
<br>
**"small and quiet as furniture"** — a figure whose two halves the reader cannot hold: furniture is not small and is not quiet, it is silent, and "small as furniture" is not true of any furniture. **Replace:** "who had been writing in the corner of the pantry all this while and had not said a word,".<br>
<br>
**"and her face closed like the door of a house"** — a weak simile competing with the actual door the whole scene is about; a face closing like a door is the dead version and "of a house" does not rescue it. **Replace with the chore, per her voice rule:** "said Mattie, and put the lamp down and squared it on the shelf."<br>
<br>
**"The questions turned the evening"** — the flat version is empty: the questions changed the evening in what direction? The clause after it is a real reason and should stay. **Replace:** "He asked his questions in the order of the door, not the order of his own wanting, because it was her record and she had made it in that order."<br>
<br>
**"A measurement that found nothing is a measurement."** — a portable closer, and the second aphorism in the scene alongside "you are its only record, and the record is good"; Edwin's budget is one. It removes no reason: the sentence after it already carries the whole argument. **Cut**, leaving: "'These two are worth as much as the figures,' he said, while he wrote. 'Most people don't write down the days nothing happened, and then in a year nobody can say when the nothing stopped.'"<br>
<br>
**"A green stack is watched the way a sick beast is watched."** — the most portable sentence in his one composed teaching paragraph, which rule 6c forbids as its ending; it is ornament, not mechanism, and the mechanism is complete without it. **Cut**, ending the paragraph on "and it takes the barn with it."<br>
<br>
### Part 4 — voice rules, countable<br>
<br>
**Mattie's number density is above Edwin's.** Counted: ~10 number-bearing tokens in ~197 words of her dialogue (≈51/1,000) against ~10–11 in ~298 of his (≈35/1,000). Her file requires her to carry fewer. The concentration is in one line — **"Both of them said fairy-rings, and one of them said it twice, and the second one laughed."** — which is also the cumulative and-pile with a running count that rule 7b assigns to <span class="redacted">redacted: character</span>. **Replace:** "The two men I asked said fairy-rings. One of them laughed." That brings her to ≈36/1,000 and removes the pile.<br>
<br>
**"A figure from a thing that moves isn't a figure."** — Part 4, banned for her by name: an aphorism/antithesis, zero tolerance, and the register is Edwin's. **Replace with an act on her own ground:** "Nobody. They don't move. I took the first one off the <span class="redacted">redacted: character</span> and then they laid the <span class="redacted">redacted: character</span>."<br>
<br>
**"When the clover stopped I dug four spits"** — finite subordinate clause, banned for her (rule 7e) and the only separator that survives against Peri. **Replace:** "The clover stopped and I dug four spits, different places, inside the arcs."<br>
<br>
**"and I'd sooner it caught," said Mattie, "because a fire I'd know what to do with."** — same ban, and the *because* clause is the reason and must be kept. **Re-punctuate, keeping the reason:** "and I'd sooner it caught," said Mattie. "A fire I'd know what to do with."<br>
<br>
**"They were fat and fine and I sold them fat and fine, and I'll not talk about it tonight."** — the doubled insistence inside a three-part and-pile; the doubling is <span class="redacted">redacted: character</span>'s. Keep the repetition, break the pile. **Replace:** "They'd grazed it four days. They were fat and fine and I sold them fat and fine. I'll not talk about it tonight."<br>
<br>
**The full-stop question is not spent anywhere in the scene**, and the stake is the sheep. Her file says it is worth everything spent once at the peak. **Add, after Peri shows her the page:** "'Is that all that goes down.' / 'That's all,' said Peri." Then the kettle.<br>
<br>
**"The sheep. The entry says they came off in Milkmonth. Where did they go?"** — Part 4 / VOICE.md "people say what they mean": Peri gives no reason for the question, and a reader cannot see why the sheep's destination matters. Supplying it inside the question also makes the ask fair rather than a poke at a grieving woman. **Replace:** "The sheep. The entry says they came off in Milkmonth, and they'd been grazing inside the arcs. Where did they go?"<br>
<br>
### Part 6 — weak where it should be strong<br>
<br>

<br>
## Part 1 — Does this sentence make sense?<br>
<br>
**"begun while Sennet's no was still fresh"** — Part 1, *dependents of every edit / check who was in the room and when*. Scene 37 ends with Sennet at the Ordish gate at five the next morning giving his three terms, and Burl already cutting the <span class="redacted">redacted: character</span> gap. A reader arriving at 38 has watched the no be reversed; "still fresh" reads as a contradiction of the scene immediately above, and it makes the rest of the scene's Sennet material (the barn three, Peri's entry) look like it has forgotten the yes. Replacement: drop the no and place the day — *"The stakes were the same day's other work, begun while Burl's first turf was still off the line beyond the <span class="redacted">redacted: character</span>, and they started with an errand that went easier than errands go."*<br>
<br>
**"the edge they would have marked was on ground where no stake of Mattie's could stand"** — Part 1, *a claim whose support was just removed*. Since Sennet has said yes, the reader needs the distinguishing fact, which the scene owns and does not say: his leave was for six spade holes, dug and turfed the same day, not for oak driven in and left ten years. Replacement: add the reason — *"Sennet had given six holes, dug and turfed inside the morning. He had not given anybody leave to drive oak into his headland and leave it there for ten years, and nobody was going to ask him for that this week."*<br>
<br>
**"*K, L, M: made and waiting. The edge they mark isn't ours to stand a stake in.*"** — Part 1, *pronoun with no clear antecedent*, plus Part 2. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> The sentence is also shaped rather than literal, which peri.md forbids her ("her best lines are literal"), and it delivers a verdict on a permission that was granted the morning before. Replacement: *"K, L, M: made and waiting. The edge they'd mark is on Mr Sennet's grass."*<br>
<br>
**"two fixed things that would outlive everybody present — the gatepost or the thorn stump, the water trough or the ditch willow"** — Part 1, *a fact overstated into a falsehood*. A water trough will not outlive Edwin, who has about a <span class="redacted">redacted: character</span>; nor reliably a willow. Name the property actually wanted. Replacement: *"two fixed things that were not going to move — the gatepost or the thorn stump, the water trough or the ditch willow, whichever pair the ground offered."*<br>
<br>
**"She looked at her mother, and then back at Edwin"** — Part 1, *check who was in the room*. Mattie is never put in the field: the party at the driving is Peri with the log, Burl with the maul, Edwin on his knees. Either place her — *"Mattie had come out at the eighth stake and stood at the <span class="redacted">redacted: character</span> end with her arms folded"* — or cut the look and keep the poker turn.<br>
<br>
**"charred at the point before it's driven ... The char is why the point doesn't rot"** — Part 1, *a cause left out / an act asserted but never performed*. The scene narrates cutting, lettering and driving in detail; the char is asserted of these stakes and never happens on the page, and the forge and the hot poker are right there. Add it to Tess's paragraph: *"She charred the points in the forge fire first, twelve of them, turning each one until the wood went black and stopped smoking, and then heated the poker and burned into each stake a letter and the date, 25 Cornmonth, deep enough to be read through ten years of weather."*<br>
<br>
**"waved away the sixpence with something close to alarm"** — Part 1, *check every number*. A sixpence is Edwin's fee token, not the price of a dozen and more seasoned oak poles; as a purchase offer it is so far under the value that refusing it is not a gift and the alarm has nothing to be alarmed at. Replacement: *"waved away the money before Edwin had got the purse the whole way out of his coat, with something close to alarm"* — no figure.<br>
<br>
**"Edwin should walk up before milking" / "Nine of the twelve went in that evening"** — Part 1, *check the clock*. Between the evening milking and the light going, the scene fits: two quarter-mile trips carrying a dozen oak poles, twelve stakes cut, twelve points charred, twelve letterings burned deep, and nine stakes driven with two chained offsets and a log entry apiece. It does not fit, and Tess milks till seven (minor.md), so she cannot be at the forge and in the field in that window. Replacement: make the errand an afternoon one — *"and that Edwin should walk up after dinner"* — and let the lettering be done by the time the milking is.<br>
<br>
**"So I know how to be wrong in writing."** — Part 1, *restate it flat and check the clauses agree*. She did not write anything; her mother did. Flat, the claim is that she knows what it is like to have a wrong thing of hers written down and checked, which is not what the sentence says. See also the Part 4 finding on this speech.<br>
<br>
**"and no O either, for the same reason, if a series ever runs that long"** — Part 1, *check the count*. O is the fourteenth letter of a series that skips I; this one is already at M after a single evening, with N owed. "If a series ever runs that long" is two stakes away, not a remote contingency. Replacement: cut the trailing conditional — *"and no O either, for the same reason."*<br>
<br>
**"the way he had bracketed it the day before"** — Part 1, *a reference the reader cannot parse*, and VOICE.md's rule that every exchange is understandable without the previous scene. "Bracketed" is a callback word carrying the meaning on its own. Replacement: *"finding the edge with his hands the way he had the day before, working in from live ground until the dead ground started under his fingers."*<br>
<br>
**"at the ninth stake, poker in hand" / "when the ninth was down"** — Part 1, *odd line on a second reading*. Two different ninths, twelve lines apart, in a scene whose whole business is counting. Replacement for the first: *"at the ninth of them, poker in hand."*<br>
<br>
**"I can keep a day."** — Part 1, *a word doing a job it cannot do*. "Keep a day" is not an idiom the reader can land on first pass. Replacement: *"I can keep to a day."*<br>
<br>
**"within an inch of the thickness Edwin would have named if anybody had asked him to name one"** — Part 1, *odd qualification*. He had named one, in his own list: oak, cut thick. The counterfactual is coy where the plain version is stronger and just as deniable. Replacement: *"and within an inch of the thickness he would have specified."*<br>
<br>
## Part 2 — The gesture at meaning<br>
<br>
**"so hear the whole of the design"** and **"that is the whole of the qualification"** — the banned family, twice in one scene: *"'the whole of it' continues to be a weird recurring phrase that you should avoid."* The first is also announce-y — a speaker telling the room a speech is coming. Replacements: cut "so hear the whole of the design" entirely (the speech works from "is the part that matters more than the driving"); and *"That," said Edwin, "is the qualification, and most people three times your age never sit it."*<br>
<br>
**"with no motion in the work that was about anything except the work"** — a sentence shaped like weight; flat, it says he worked steadily. It also spends narrator budget the scene needs elsewhere. Replacement: *"inside the hour, and did not put the axe down once in it."*<br>
<br>
## Part 3 — Would this exist in a real world?<br>
<br>
**"most people three times your age never sit it"** — the ages rule. Three times fifteen is forty-five, which in this world is nobody's marker of long life or long practice. It survives if it is read as "grown men", but it is close to the fault. If it stays, it should not also be carrying the scene's closing weight; see the Part 5 note below.<br>
<br>
## Part 4 — Why would this character say this?<br>
<br>
**"So I know how to be wrong in writing. That's the qualification, isn't it."** — Part 4, *a line that comes from the plot's need and not the want*, and Part 5, *asserted, not shown*. Tess coins the abstraction, names the term ("qualification") before Edwin has used it, and hands him his own verdict to echo. minor.md's Tess is schoolroom-direct and concrete ("It's a field. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> Keep every reason in the speech — the stump, Seedmonth, her mother's note — and let her stay in the concrete: *"I'll do it. I said the stump had moved, in Seedmonth, and it hadn't — it was the reading that was wrong, and I'd said it in front of Mother and two men. She wrote* ask if stump moved *in the book instead of laughing at me. I don't mind a wrong thing of mine being written down."* Edwin then supplies "That is the qualification," which is his job and his register.<br>
<br>
**Edwin's composed teaching paragraphs: four.** The I-omission speech, the char speech, the design speech, and "Somebody who'll do it on the day". edwin.md 6c allows one per scene, and Part 5's *recitation that should be conversation* applies to the design speech, which is the longest single block in the scene and is delivered at two people who say nothing during it. Do not shorten it — the content is the instrument and every clause of it is a reason. Break it into an exchange: give Tess "What does somebody actually do with it, then, every month?" after "A stake does two jobs," and give Peri the practical question that pulls out the *not reached* rule ("What do I write against a stake the edge hasn't got to?"). Same words, three mouths, and the budget is spent once.<br>
<br>
## Part 5 — Is the device working?<br>
<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> It is Edwin's licence, it is the thesis of the instrument, and it earns its place; keep it. The other two portable closers ("the whole of the qualification…never sit it", "no motion in the work…") are handled above, and with those two plainer this one is inside budget.<br>

## SCENE 39 — "Read it as six"<br>
<br>
### Part 1<br>
<br>
**"registers coming from fifty miles off that I haven't seen"** — Part 1, *check every count*. Contradicted eleven lines later by his own post arithmetic in the same scene: "Four days to Wystow if the roads are kind, six if they're not." A carrier does not take four to six days over fifty miles, and Appleford is "forty miles away" in this same speech, so fifty reads as nearer than Sorrel. Replacement: "registers coming from a hundred miles off that I haven't seen".<br>
<br>
**"I'll reckon on an answer the twelfth day and be pleasantly surprised or not surprised."** — Part 1, *check every inference*. The figures he has just given are 4+4 at best, 6+6 at worst, *plus* "something at their end takes what it takes". Twelve is therefore the bad roads with zero days at Wystow — the one combination he has just said he cannot count on. Replacement: "I'll reckon on an answer in a fortnight and be pleasantly surprised or not surprised." (Peri's next line then becomes "A fortnight.")<br>
<br>
**"Peri, who was mending the day's pages across the table"** — Part 1, *a word doing a job it cannot do*. "Mending" pages means repairing torn paper; what she is doing is correcting or fair-copying the day's entries. Replacement: "who was making up the day's pages across the table".<br>
<br>
**"this is the other half of a handover, and you saw the first half in the tower kitchen, the night the lists were made"** — Part 1, *a reference with no clear antecedent*; VOICE §2 *callback phrases never carry meaning on their own*. A reader who has not read scene 19 gets nothing from "the first half" or "the night the lists were made", and the speech then builds on it. Elaborate: "…you saw the first half in the tower kitchen the night before I left, when she and I cut the vale's work into two lists and I gave her the longer one and did not ask to see it again." (Also worth checking against 19 that the room is the kitchen and not the study — 05 puts their list business in the study.)<br>
<br>
**"a week like this one"** — Part 1, *check the clock*. Day 11; he crossed the Ashway on Day 7 and reached Fenholt on Day 9. Five days, not a week. Replacement: "after the week he'd had" → "after the days he'd had", or name it: "in five days like these".<br>
<br>
**"you saw … the night the lists were made" / "I have broken exactly one written date in three hundred years"** — the second is fine against canon (110 years ago, a coast turn, eleven parishes); no change. Flagged only because the brief asked the count be checked.<br>
<br>
### Part 2<br>
<br>
**Narrator close: "on the grounds that other people's letters, like other people's ground, want leave before digging."** — Part 2, *the gesture at meaning*, and it over-frames the beat exactly as the brief suspects. Canon requires the no-letters-home thread to stay small, instant and unremarked (`peri.md`, <span class="redacted">redacted: character</span> ruling); a closing figure that compares her privacy to a field being dug tells the reader the beat is significant and supplies a reading she has not earned. It is also the scene's only narrator aphorism spent on a decoration. Not a cut of a reason — a cut of a gloss. Replacement, keeping a plain reason: "and Edwin put the wax away and did not ask, because it was hers to send or not."<br>
<br>
**"a finding with one name on it can be buried with the name"** (inside the Wystow letter) — Part 2 / Edwin 6c. Fifth portable closer in the scene (see the budget finding below). Plain version: "One name is easy to lose. Four are not."<br>
<br>
### Part 5 — the aphorism budget (the scene's largest finding)<br>
<br>
Edwin gets **one composed teaching paragraph, one aphorism, and one contrastive pair per scene** (`edwin.md` 6c, 13; `voices.md`). This scene runs **four composed paragraphs and seven portable closers**. Survivors and replacements:<br>
<br>
**KEEP (the aphorism): "a written date gets altered while it's still alive."** It is the scene's spine, it is his canon rule, and it is the lesson he has explicitly framed as one.<br>
<br>
**KEEP (the contrastive pair): "You hand work to somebody entitled to alter it, or you haven't handed it over at all."** — but it currently *closes* the paragraph, and 6c forbids the last sentence being the most portable one. Move it to the head of the paragraph ("Sorrel doesn't need my reasons. You hand work to somebody entitled to alter it, or you haven't handed it over at all. She'll build better ones…") and end the paragraph on the fact: "…she'd be making them in Fallowmonth weather with Fallowmonth facts, and I'd be making them tonight with none."<br>
<br>
**CUT/REPLACE — "You've just made them your clerk at a distance"** — Part 5, *redundancy*: it restates the pair immediately after it. Cut the sentence.<br>
<br>
**CUT/REPLACE — "It's by altering your dates early, when the world alters, and never once pretending the old date is still alive when you know it's dead."** — Part 5, *redundancy* (it says the aphorism again, in the same alive/dead figure) plus a second portable closer. Replacement, plain and ending on fact: "You alter the date the day the world alters, and you say in the letter what altered it. That's the whole of it."<br>
<br>
**CUT/REPLACE — "A man who signs alone once is brave. A man who's allowed to keep signing alone is being spent by people who won't put their own names at risk"** — Part 5 / `edwin.md` 4: anaphora, two clauses of equal length, and the most portable line in the speech; all three are the named defect. Replacement: "Signing alone once is brave. Being left to do it a second time means nobody above him is willing to be wrong in public, and I've watched colleges do that to their best clerks for three centuries."<br>
<br>
**KEEP but plainen — "The letters work. Slowly, which is the same speed as fields."** The callback to scene 13 is right and it answers her question; the trailing clause is a third aphorism. Replacement: "The letters work," said Edwin. "They work slowly. Nothing in that envelope will be less true in twelve days, and if it would be, it isn't ready to send."<br>
<br>
**"I'd sooner lose a hand than do that to her."** — Part 4 / `edwin.md` 4, *at a peak he is plainer and factual about himself*. Hyperbole is not in his register anywhere in canon, and it lands oddly on a man whose arm is about to matter. Replacement: "and I have never once done that to her, and I'm not going to start at forty miles."<br>
<br>
### Part 6<br>
<br>
**Em-dash budget.** 11 in 1,147 words = 9.6 per 1,000 against a budget of ~2. Priority removals: line 11's three (the "Everything else — … — is hers" pair becomes a colon list or a separate sentence; "Sorrel doesn't need my reasons — she'll build" becomes a full stop); line 19's four (the "second hand" apposition takes commas; "and — he underlined it — *converted*" becomes "and, underlined, *converted*"). Keep line 29's "You just —" (Peri's false start, which the rule protects) and one pair at most elsewhere.<br>
<br>
### Not findings, recorded because the brief asked<br>
<br>
The quoted letters read correctly as record-voice (the Sorrel note in particular). Peri's grammar is within her rules: one dash, performed as a false start; no aphorism; "That's two sentences" is her literal register; the three-fold "You told the rider / me / Sorrel" is repetition, not a balanced antithesis.<br>

**"and wrote that down too, under the three pictures, where a reader in ten years would find her being honest, and closed the book on the pencil."** — Part 2, *the gesture at meaning*, and it is a narrator gloss telling the reader that Peri is admirable at the exact moment she has just been plain. "A reader in ten years" is also Edwin's own warrant construction (`edwin.md` 2) applied to her by the narration. Cut the clause: "…and wrote that down too, under the three pictures, and closed the book on the pencil." The scene then ends on her verdict, which VOICE §2 permits.<br>
<br>
### Part 5<br>
<br>
**"One: a live worm at the deep spit … Two: Burl's spade came off the <span class="redacted">redacted: character</span>-ditch spoil"** — Part 5, *the "Item:" tic*. A numbered list inside dialogue is the same LLM verbal tic as "Item:". Keep both hypotheses; drop the labels: "A live worm at the deep spit, twelve yards, which if it lives there means the ground at that depth is not what the census says two feet higher. And Burl's spade came off the <span class="redacted">redacted: character</span>-ditch spoil between the eight and the twelve" — Burl … — "and a worm can ride a spade."<br>
<br>
**"A finding with its doubt written next to it is worth ten findings without, because the reader can see what it would take to knock it down."** — `edwin.md` 6c: the composed teaching paragraph's last sentence may not be a proverb or the most portable line in it. The *reason* stays (per the standing caution); only the proverbial frame goes. Replacement: "…and the words *possibly carried* go against the worm in letters as big as the finding, because a reader can then see what it would take to knock it down. Without that he can only take my word for it, and he'd be right not to."<br>
<br>
**"It looks like the truth. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> **Keep.**<br>
<br>
**Sennet: "There's no story the parish can tell about stakes it never sees. And when the day comes that it wants telling, the stakes'll say I knew, and staked it, and wasn't afraid of my own grass. That's the story, and it's mine, and it'll keep."** — Part 4 / `minor.md`. Three problems: the opening is a shaped portable sentence (Edwin's construction in Sennet's mouth); "I knew, and staked it, and wasn't afraid" plus "it's mine, and it'll keep" is the inventory pile joined by *and*, which canon assigns to <span class="redacted">redacted: character</span>; and the last third re-delivers the third term he already stated at length in scene 37 (Part 5, *redundancy*). His register is short, flat, and dares you. Replacement: "'Nobody's seen them go in,' he said, 'so there's nothing for anybody to be telling yet. When it wants telling, the stakes say I knew and I staked it. That'll do me.'"<br>
<br>
**Peri's rate speech, "and"-count** — `peri.md` 1, hard cap of two "and"s per speech; this runs three ("the census is you, **and** you leave"; "a stake in the ground **and** a hope"). Replacement for the last: "That's a stake in the ground, with a hope of a second reading against it."<br>
<br>
**"There's nothing to see here — no band, no colour, nothing."** — `peri.md` 6a, the countable clause: a joining dash anywhere in her dialogue reduces her false start to zero, and "until — no." is the real one. The joining dash is a full stop. Replacement: "There's nothing to see here. No band, no colour, nothing."<br>
<br>
**"and the census is you, and you leave"** — compressed to code; elaborate per VOICE §2. Replacement: "The census found this edge, and the census is a thing only you can do, and you're leaving."<br>
<br>
**"habits are what a clever man would show an instrument"** — Part 4 / `peri.md`: she does not produce aphorisms or generic present-tense claims about how the world works; her best lines are literal. Replacement: "because if he is what I think, habits are the only thing he'd let an instrument see."<br>
<br>
### Part 6<br>
<br>
**Em-dash budget.** 10 in 1,404 words = 7.1 per 1,000 against ~2. Protected: "until — no." (her false start) and the Burl interruption pair on line 15. Remove: line 21's "like a fair" dash (the clause is cut anyway); line 23's pair (recast as sentences with the mechanism finding above); line 39's joining dash; line 45's "*Who is the priest of Fenholt?* — who-questions" (full stop); line 53's "*is he mine* — and I couldn't" (full stop). That lands it at three.<br>
<br>
### Not findings, recorded because the brief asked<br>
<br>
Station count (6), the near-three/far-three split, the three depths, 14/11/9, and the three-wordings discipline all hold. Viewpoint holds elsewhere. The Weave answers are canon-format.<br>
<br>
---<br>
<br>
**The device a second round would be about, scene 39:** Edwin teaching in four consecutive composed paragraphs with a portable line at the end of each. Even with the six replacements above, the scene's shape is a man delivering four lessons at a girl across a table, and it will draw the *"it would flow better as a conversation"* comment that the four measurements drew. To make it work: give Peri the second and third lessons to *start* — she has just watched him break his own stated date, and she is the one who should say "you told three people two weeks", after which he answers rather than instructs. To remove it: cut the co-signer paragraph entirely and let the letter's read-aloud line stand alone with Peri's "He'll take that badly" and his two-sentence answer.<br>
<br>
**The device a second round would be about, scene 40:** the scene has three separate closers in its last third — Sennet's stake speech, Peri's rate speech, and the Weave beat, each ending on a verdict. It reads as three scene-endings in a row. To make it work: run the rate speech *during* the filling of the holes, before Sennet drives L and M, so the morning ends once. To remove it: move the noon Weave beat to the head of scene 41 <span class="redacted">unpublished</span>.&lt;/result&gt;<br>

<p>**Blocks the schedule surviving:**</p>

<p>Plan recorded and committed (`plans/publishing.md`, <span class="redacted">unpublished</span> 58a9a01). Now the two things you asked me to bring you:</p>
<p>## Series title shortlist</p>
<p><span class="redacted">redacted sentence: Edwin's death/mortality</span></p>
<p>1. **The Green Archmage** *(my recommendation)* — it's what the world calls Edwin, it's honest about the content (agriculture, magic, one very big fish), it's discoverable on RR, and it survives the ending: the series is about what the Green Archmage leaves behind as much as who he is. "The Green Archmage — Book One: The Wrong Man" reads like a real serial.<br>
2. **How to Save the World** — the destiny nearly verbatim ("show Peri Millbrook how to save the world") and both anchors in four words; wry in exactly the book's register. Risk: reads generic/self-help out of context, and it tips the ironic frame slightly earlier than the prose does.<br>
3. **Fieldwork** — the double meaning is the whole book (farming + investigation + the work being in fields, not towers). Elegant, but weak RR signal; readers browsing LitRPG won't know what it is.<br>
4. **Agent of Fate** — Peri's class, LitRPG-legible, and correctly names whose story the serial ultimately is. Risk: promises her genre-brained reading of the story rather than the actual one, and Book 1 is Edwin-first.<br>
5. **The World Always Needs Saving** — anchor 1 straight. Strong thesis, but it states the theme, which the voice rules say the page never does; a title arguably counts.<br>
6. **The Wrong Man** promoted to series title, with Book 1 renamed — mentioning for completeness; I think it's better spent where it is.</p>
<p>## Blurb draft</p>
<p>&gt; Peri Millbrook is eighteen, has a sword she bought new, a unique class — [Agent of Fate] — and a destiny to save the world. Her insight magic found her the greatest archmage alive to help her do it: most lives saved, most students taught, longest range, largest mana pool, every record true and none of them close. So she walked to his tower and bound his fate to hers before he could finish explaining *why* they're all true: he has spent three hundred years teaching farmers. He blesses crops. He schedules the weather. He has cast one battle spell in his life, aged eighteen, and it missed.<br>
&gt;<br>
&gt; Now Edwin Moss is out of his tower for the first time in decades, walking east behind a girl who thinks there's a dark lord somewhere, and finding — because he can't help looking — that something is quietly wrong with the ground.<br>
&gt;<br>
&gt; A slow, dry, competent fantasy about farming, teaching, measurement, and saving the world as a maintenance problem. LitRPG on her side of the page; the System mostly leaves him alone.<br>
&gt;<br>
&gt; ---<br>
&gt;<br>
&gt; *This story is openly AI-generated (tagged accordingly), written under human editorial direction. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> Chapters daily.*</p>
<p>Notes on the draft: the disclosure paragraph doubles as the "author's notes are part of the story" flag in the one place every reader passes; "it missed" stops short of the concept doc's full joke to leave chapter 1 something. Happy to tune register up or down.</p>
<p>Rule on the title (and any blurb edits), and I'll get on with the mechanical track — the markdown→RR converter and the browser-automation workflow — which doesn't depend on either.</p>
</div>

<blockquote><p><strong>David:</strong><br>
&lt;task-notification&gt;<br>
&lt;task-id&gt;ac6789311bd50ce6d&lt;/task-id&gt;<br>
&lt;tool-use-id&gt;<span class="redacted">redacted: private</span>&lt;/tool-use-id&gt;<br>

&lt;summary&gt;Agent "Persona lens on author notes" finished&lt;/summary&gt;<br>
&lt;note&gt;A task-notification fires each time this agent stops with no live background children of its own. The user can send it another message and resume it, so the same task-id may notify more than once.&lt;/note&gt;<br>
&lt;result&gt;Findings report. Facts verified against standard references where I was confident; flagged only what I can defend.<br>
<br>
**1. `01-pellings-half-past-twelve-soft.md`** (210 w; 133 + 77)<br>
- VOICE (page-pointing, 2 clauses + device annotation): "Nobody in this chapter is doing anything to the rain that the moors would not do; the argument is only about where and when." This is the note reaching up to tell the reader how to read the scene's magic, and the note already has its pointer implicitly. Proposed: cut the sentence entirely; end on "…mostly a map of which way the weather arrives."<br>
- BUDGET: the **Shearing** note is 77 words — past "a sentence or three" for a short. Proposed trim: "Two real constraints. Fleece has to be stored dry: a damp clip heats and rots in the stack and is worth nothing. And a sheep that has just lost its coat has lost its weather-proofing with it; a cold rain on freshly shorn sheep can kill them, in summer, in numbers."<br>
- FACT: orographic rainfall, damp-clip spoilage, and post-shearing cold mortality all check out.<br>
<br>
**2. `02-a-war-horse-on-a-milk-cart.md`** (129 w)<br>
- FACT (ok): the doubling rule is Harrington's — life doubles per 1% moisture removed and per ~5 °C cooler. "Every point of moisture" and "every few degrees" is a fair statement of it, and it is correctly hedged as a rule of thumb.<br>
- VOICE (overstated + a second page-pointer): "which is why every farmhouse older than the seed trade has a shelf that looks exactly like Edwin's." "Every farmhouse" is unsupportable, and the note's one pointing clause is better spent nowhere. Proposed: "A dry jar in a cool kitchen will keep beans good for sowing for years, which is why farmhouses had a shelf of them."<br>
- Otherwise clean; the closing autobiographical line is said straight (matches the logged ch. 02 claim).<br>
<br>
**3. `03-until-fulfilled.md`** (130 w)<br>
- FACT (overstated): "there was a statute about it in 1531". The 1531 statute (23 Hen. VIII c. 5, the Statute of Bridges) is about **bridges** and their repair, not fords. Proposed: "Parliament legislated on who must repair bridges in 1531 and the arguments carried on regardless."<br>
- FACT (overstated): "one of the oldest continuously running arguments in the historical record" — unverifiable as stated. Proposed: "is very old and very well documented."<br>
- VOICE (negation-then-colon, close cousin of the banned "not X but Y"): "A ford is not a fixed thing: rivers move their gravel about". Proposed: "Rivers move their gravel about, so a crossing has to be maintained or it quietly stops being a crossing."<br>
<br>
**4. `04-four-minutes.md`** (156 w, single long note, over the ~150 cap)<br>
- VOICE (banned "not X but Y"): "A heap is not a pile of rubbish rotting; it is a very large number of living things eating". Proposed: "A heap is a very large number of living things eating, and everything in how you build one follows from keeping them fed and breathing."<br>
- VOICE (world content + second page-pointer): "Everything the class does before anyone casts is exactly what you would do at home, and it works." References casting, i.e. the magic, and annotates the scene's construction. Proposed: cut; end on "Left to itself a well-built heap takes a season or so."<br>
- The Widow Sepp clause is then the note's one permitted pointer and can stay.<br>
- FACT: 60 °C+ thermophilic peak, wrung-out-cloth moisture, turning for aeration, anaerobic souring — all correct.<br>
<br>
**5. `05-in-some-sort-of-order.md`** (151 w)<br>
- VOICE (page-pointing beyond one clause + story annotation): "Given one favour to hand out, there is no real decision to make, which does not make the letter to the other parish any nicer to write." This narrates the chapter's dilemma and its magic economy from below the line. Proposed: end at "Late is a smaller harvest. Wet is no harvest."<br>
- FACT: leaching of soluble sugars from rained-on swath, extended field-drying, stack moulding, and the roots-vs-hay contrast are all sound.<br>
<br>
**6. `06-the-front-from-the-roof.md`** (131 w)<br>
- FACT (overstated, moderate confidence): "why a moor can take the worst of a front and give it back to the rivers over days instead of hours". Blanket bog is usually at or near saturation and its runoff response is characteristically *flashy*; the flood-buffering claim is contested in the peatland-hydrology literature and does not hold for the intact bog as stated. Proposed: "Sphagnum holds many times its own weight in water, which is why the ground up there is wet even in a dry spell, and why the walking is the way it is."<br>
- FACT (overstated): "moorland makes its own weather in a small way; all that held water has to evaporate off." Evaporation from sphagnum-dominated bog is comparatively *low* — the moss restricts it. Proposed: cut the sentence.<br>
- VOICE: clean otherwise; the curlew decline is accurate and the closing line reads as feeling, not a wink.<br>
<br>
**7. `07-can-you-actually-fight.md`** (181 w; 121 + 60)<br>
- VOICE (world content — the note explains a charm): "The tests Edwin's charm is standing in for are all real." Proposed: "The tests are all real."<br>
- VOICE (unsupported swipe): "it is worth saying plainly because the books get it wrong". No books are named and I cannot verify the claim. Proposed: "and it is worth saying plainly:".<br>
- VOICE (floating epigram, and it points at the magic): "Four nights is four nights, whatever bought them." Proposed: cut; end on "somebody awake noticing when things change."<br>
- FACT: iodine–starch ripeness testing, pressing cheese, biting a grain for hardness, and pre-store condition governing keeping quality are all correct. Autobiographical line matches the logged ch. 07 claim.<br>
<br>
**8. `08-what-youre-standing-in.md`** (162 w, single long note, over the ~150 cap)<br>
- VOICE (banned "not X but Y", in the final position): "and the difference was farming, not weather." Also weak as fact — the Irish famine turned on a pathogen (*Phytophthora infestans*) reaching a monoculture, plus policy, more than on farming practice generally. Proposed: "England had its last true famine some two centuries before its nearest neighbour had its worst one, and what had changed in between was the farming."  Better still, given the fact problem: replace the whole final sentence with "What ended it was that, over about two centuries, and unevenly."<br>
- VOICE (three page-pointing clauses where one is allowed): "Everything Edwin says here is a plain description of the pre-modern world"; "It killed exactly as the chapter says"; "it ended the same way it ends here, without anyone slaying anything". Keep the first, cut the other two: "It killed the old first, then the smallest children, quietly, parish by parish. And it ended without anyone slaying anything…" — or, to lose the device annotation as well, "And it ended through better rotations, better winter feed, better roads to move grain from where it grew to where it failed."<br>
- FACT (ok as hedged): 1315–17 following two rain-drowned harvests, ~10% mortality in parts of northern Europe, and the 1690s "ill years" range of roughly 1-in-20 to 1-in-8 in worst-hit areas are all within the accepted estimates, and "the registers that survive suggest" is the right level of confidence.<br>
<br>
**9. `09-the-cart-up-the-hill.md`** (140 w)<br>
- VOICE (story annotation from below the line): "The detail in this chapter I most want to vouch for is the shed at the bridge." This is the author commenting on their own craft choices. Proposed: "Anything travelling off the main rounds waited until somebody happened to be going that way…"<br>
- VOICE (floating epigram): "The system worked. It just worked on its own clock." Proposed: cut, or fold into the fact — "It worked, on its own clock, and everybody planned around the clock."<br>
- VOICE (list with a swerve on the last item, twice): "taking goods, parcels, shopping errands and the occasional passenger" and "the postal service, the delivery firm and half the news network at once". Keep one at most. Proposed for the second: "They were the postal service and the delivery firm at once, and a good deal of the news."<br>
- FACT: carriers' fixed rounds and fixed days, and the reconstruction of regional economies from carriers' lists in trade directories, are both sound. "Complaints about parcels sitting for weeks… a fixture of the period's letters" is plausible but unverifiable as stated; consider "and the complaints about it are easy to find."<br>
<br>
**10. `10-more-work-than-the-day-needed.md`** (199 w; 145 + 54)<br>
- VOICE (floating epigram): "Thirty years of one book beats a map." Follows a sentence that already says it. Proposed: cut, and let "each farm's own record predicts its frosts better than any theory of the district" close the note.<br>
- FACT: radiation frost, cold-air drainage and pooling, the saving effect of cloud or wind, valley-floor damage, and hen's-egg hail on this island (Horsham 1958 among others) all check out; slate damage at that size is real. Autobiographical line matches the logged ch. 10 claim.<br>
- BUDGET: within the ~300 total; the long note at 145 is at the edge of the cap.<br>
<br>
Cross-cutting:<br>
- Em-dash budget is clean throughout — every file uses the dash only in the bold heading, as the format requires.<br>
- No "fun fact", no credential-offering, no self-deprecation about the enthusiasm anywhere. The persona's autobiographical lines (02, 07, 10) are all said straight and all agree with the log in `notes/author.md`.<br>
- None of the files carry the `---` / `*Author's note.*` opener from `notes/farming.md` "Format of an author's note". Given the README describes these as standalone per-chapter files assembled by the commenter and uploader, this is probably intentional, but the two documents disagree and one should be updated.<br>
- Clean-ish files: none are fully clean. **02** and **06** are closest — 02 needs only the "every farmhouse" trim; 06's problems are factual (peat hydrology) rather than voice.&lt;/result&gt;<br>

&lt;/task-notification&gt;</p></blockquote>

Contamination is one finding about the whole scene, not four sentence-level fixes — the scene needs redrafting with the groundwork chapter shut, not patching.\n\n### `draft/book-01/scenes/39-read-it-as-six.md`\n\n| Quote | Rule | Why |\n|---|---|---|\n| \"One name is easy to lose; four are not.\" | `aphorism-budget` | Fully portable epigram detached from the particulars, in a scene already over budget. |\n| \"The letters work. They work slowly. Nothing in that envelope will be less true in twelve days, and if it would be, it isn't ready to send.\" | `aphorism-budget` | Third portable philosophical cap, landing as the scene's near-final beat where cleverness should be near zero. |\n| \"You hand work to somebody entitled to alter it, or you haven't handed it over at all.\" | `aphorism-budget` (judge-added) | Fourth portable epigram, earlier in the same Edwin speech — the clearest evidence the scene is well over one-per-scene. |\n| \"Nothing in that envelope will be less true in twelve days, and if it would be, it isn't ready to send.\" | `maxim-capped-speech` | Second maxim-capped turn in the scene, and it is the closing line: the scene ends on a cadence rather than a cut. |\n\n**Scene-level verdict:** the aphorism budget is not overspent by one line but by three. Borderlines\n\n- **02 / `missing-qualifier`** — \"It's called the Pellings.\" Proper noun with no word saying what it is (farm, valley, parish). Context carries it; one qualifier would settle it. Cheap fix, low stakes.\n- **02 / `unearned-authority`** — \"Don't ask it *who*… Ask it *what*.\" Imperatives to a girl who has not agreed to be taught, but they concern her own question and she refuses them immediately, so the page pays for it.\n\nBoth are judgement calls the lint is right to surface and wrong to auto-fail.\n\n## 3. False-positive rate per rule\n\n| Rule | Fired | Confirmed | FP | FP rate |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| `contamination-overlap` | 4 | 4 | 0 | 0% |\n| `description-list` | 1 | 1 | 0 | 0% |\n| `aphorism-budget` | 3 | 2 | 1 | 33% |\n| `maxim-capped-speech` | 2 | 1 | 1 | 50% |\n| `missing-qualifier` | 1 | 0 (borderline) | 0 | — |\n| `unearned-authority` | 1 | 0 (borderline) | 0 | — |\n| `canon-scale-contradiction` | 3 | 0 | 3 | **100%** |\n| `announce-before-deliver` | 1 | 0 | 1 | **100%** |\n| `cause-left-out` | 1 | 0 | 1 | **100%** |\n| `implication-stopped-short` | 1 | 0 | 1 | **100%** |\n\n**Needs counter_examples urgently:**\n\n- **`canon-scale-contradiction`** — 3/3 false. It cannot tell an *age* from a *duration*. Counter-examples needed: \"three hundred years\" as the length of the Pellings work or of a habit; \"three hundred years\" as canon's own round figure for Edwin's working life beside his age of 350; \"three centuries\" as a consistent synonym. Better still, narrow the rule to fire only when the number is bound to a person by an age-marker (\"he was\", \"aged\", \"years old\").\n- **`announce-before-deliver`** — the scanner is matching continuous clause chains. The rule targets a *bare naming fragment* (\"Silence.\") preceding the delivering sentence. Counter-example: \"…and said nothing else, and Edwin put the wax away and did not ask.\" Add a precondition that the announcing unit be a fragment with no finite verb.\n- **`cause-left-out`** and **`implication-stopped-short`** — both fired on author's notes where the cause/implication is stated in the *adjacent* sentence. Both need a look-behind/look-ahead window of one sentence before flagging, plus the two pilot quotes as counter-examples.\n- **`aphorism-budget` / `maxim-capped-speech`** — these do not over-fire so much as mis-*rank*. Both correctly detected overspend and then nominated the line that should be kept. They need a \"which one survives\" step: flag the set, mark the most particular / least portable member as the keeper, and report the rest.\n\n## 4. Constructions confirmed 2+ times → propose a regex-tier rule\n\nOnly one construction clears the bar: **`contamination-overlap`**, confirmed 4× in one file (26 runs). It is already tooled by `tools/overlap_check.py` and should be promoted from a judged lens to a hard `tier=regex`-class gate — it needs no judgement at all, and its judged FP rate is 0%.\n\nThe gate is not a regex over prose but an exact-run test against the deleted-draft corpus; the regex tier's role is normalisation:\n\n```python\nimport re\n\n# tier=regex, rule_id=\"contamination-overlap\"\n# Normalise a scene to a lowercase word stream, then test every 8-gram\n# against the n-gram set built from the deleted drafts in git history\n# (chapters/, rewrite/, draft/book-01/rejected-act2/).\n\nWORD_RE = re.compile(r\"[a-z0-9']+\")\nNGRAM_N = 8\n\ndef ngrams(text: str, n: int = NGRAM_N):\n    words = WORD_RE.findall(text.lower())\n    for i in range(len(words) - n + 1):\n        yield \" \".join(words[i:i + n])\n\ndef contamination_runs(scene_text: str, corpus_ngrams: frozenset[str]) -&amp;gt; list[str]:\n    return [g for g in ngrams(scene_text) if g in corpus_ngrams]\n```\n\nNotes for implementation: report at scene level (a run count and a sample), never as N independent sentence findings — the pilot shows the sentence framing misleads, since \"She was wearing fate magic.\" is under eight words and is only contaminated as part of a longer run. Suggested threshold: any run at all is a finding; ≥5 runs means redraft with the source shut rather than edit.\n\nThe `aphorism-budget` family fired 2+ times as well but is inherently a semantic count, not a surface pattern; it stays a judged lens with a keeper-selection step added.\n\n## 5. Did haiku-level hunting work?\n\nMixed, and the split is informative. Of 19 candidates, judges confirmed 8 and rejected 9 — a bare-majority rejection rate, which on its own would look like a failing hunter. But the rejections are concentrated almost entirely in four rules that fail for one shared reason: the scanner reads a quote without its neighbouring sentence. `canon-scale-contradiction` cannot see that the sentence before names a habit rather than an age; `cause-left-out` and `implication-stopped-short` cannot see that the next sentence supplies exactly what they claim is missing. 2. Key Technical Concepts:<br>
   - Three-layer lint pipeline: deterministic (regex+metric) → haiku hunters (semantic rules by lens) → opus judges (CONFIRMED/FALSE-POSITIVE/BORDERLINE per file) → opus synthesis (report, FP analysis, proposed rules).<br>
   - rules.json schema: {id, name, tier: regex|metric|semantic, scope: [scene|note|plan], pattern/flags (regex), metric {kind: rate|word-count|corpus-count|count-outside-heading|unwrapped-paragraphs|word-recurrence, ...; rate has per/max/token|pattern, optional level:"corpus", min_count floor default 2}, severity error|warn, description, why, source, examples, counter_examples, lens (semantic), allowed (regex-tier whitelist of exact substrings that suppress matches within ±120 chars)}.<br>
   - Lenses (semantic rules): closers(7), wit(10), sense(8), dialogue(11), narration(12), notes(4), world(6). ~102→104 rules total.<br>
   - Workflow patterns: pipeline(FILES, huntStage, judgeStage) with parallel haiku hunts inside stage 1; JSON schemas FIND_SCHEMA {findings:[{rule_id,quote,reason}]} and JUDGE_SCHEMA {verdicts:[{rule_id,quote,verdict,reason,keeper_note}], judge_added:[...]}; models explicit (haiku hunt, opus judge/synthesis).<br>
   - Pilot learnings baked into skill + prompts: hunters must read one sentence either side before flagging; budget rules nominate a KEEPER (most particular/least portable); contamination-overlap is tooled (tools/overlap_check.py), never an agent lens; word census = judge information.<br>
   - Skill autonomy policy: regex/metric errors fixed mechanically pre-presentation/upload; CONFIRMED semantic → editing process (main session for Book 1); BORDERLINE → unresolved commenter comments; proposed rules → tools/lint/proposed-rules.md, never into rules.json without a ruling; FP rejections become counter_examples.<br>
   - RR HTTP: requests Session from tools/rr/.auth/cookies.json + UA; form_token(html, action) regex; multipart POST with repeated genres/tags/contentWarnings tuples; CoverUpload file field; edit form at /author-dashboard/submissions/edit/222993 accepts full re-POST.<br>
   - Commenter comments JSON schema: {file, comments:[{id, created (ISO), body, resolved, anchor?:{line,col,endLine,endCol,quote,prefix,suffix}}]} at comments/&lt;rel&gt;.comments.json; whole-doc comment = no anchor. tools/commenter/new_comments.py [--ack] lists/acks unseen comments.<br>
<br>
3. Files and Code Sections:<br>
   - `tools/commenter/index.html` — added navBar(where) using DOCS order; .chnav CSS; bars at top and bottom of render(); buttons disabled at ends; committed 26f1329.<br>

Compiled by opus agent from llm-tells/voices/author/VOICE + comments history; normalized (metric kinds unified, 10 uncountable metrics → semantic with `budget` field kept, lenses assigned); enriched over the session with counter_examples (canon-scale-contradiction age-vs-duration; announce-before-deliver fragment precondition; cause-left-out/implication-stopped-short adjacent-sentence window; triad-swerve/missing-qualifier/long-build-short-drop/aphorism-budget from ch01-10 FPs; negative-parallelism taproom line; cute-cryptic-closer iodine calibration), `allowed` whitelist on negative-parallelism ("the eel men not being a quiet sort, but"), new rules `ill-not` (regex error, `I['’]ll not\b`) and `course-for-class` (regex WARN, `\b(?:the|every|a|his|each)\s+course\b` flags i, description carries the diegetic-course caveat + counter_examples "his grandmother had taken the course at the crossroads", "course of the river/morning"), em-dash-budget now {kind:"count-outside-heading", token:"—", max:0} severity error all scopes.<br>
   - `tools/lint/prose_lint.py` — engine: run_regex_rule (with `allowed` suppression), run_metric_rule (rate w/ min_count floor + corpus level, word-count min/max, count-outside-heading, unwrapped-paragraphs, word-recurrence with proper-noun exclusion via caps&gt;lowers counters + STOPWORDS), lint_corpus (corpus-count + corpus-level rates aggregate n and words across invocation), body_text() strips heading lines before counting, scope inferred from path, --json/--list-rules/--tier/--scope flags, exit 1 on errors.<br>

<p>✅ 1 lines, 299 chars — 03bd6ba Chapter uploader: rr.py plan/go commands - 2-11 publish immediately, 12-40 scheduled daily at release_time_utc (17:00), top-notes uploaded verbatim iff present, form fields discovered and asse…</p>

<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>The uploader is built, dry-run tested, and committed (03bd6ba). Where things stand:</p>
<p>**The script** (`tools/rr/rr.py`):<br>
- `plan` - prints the release plan: chapters **2-11 publish immediately**, **12-40 scheduled daily at 17:00 UTC** starting the day after go (dates computed at run time, so waiting costs nothing). Verified against all 40 scenes: every payload converts cleanly, titles correct, notes attached, zero em dashes.<br>
- `go [--yes] [--only N]` - runs only *after* you press Launch (it currently refuses with "the fiction is not live yet", which I confirmed live - it will never touch the Launch button itself). On first run it fetches the real chapter form, prints the discovered field names, and aborts before posting if anything's unexpected; then uploads in order, verifying each response, stopping dead on the first validation error, and recording everything in `publishing/rr-map.json` (re-runnable: already-uploaded chapters are skipped). Top notes from `publishing/top-notes/NNN.md` go up verbatim iff present; bottom notes are the drafted author's notes.<br>
- One honest caveat: the publish/schedule field names can't be debugged until the fiction exists, so the sequence after you launch is: `go --only 2 --yes` as the careful first test, I check the live chapter, then the rest. The scheduling-field assertion means it can't silently misfire.</p>
<p>**Best release time**: my recommendation is **17:00 UTC** (1pm US Eastern, 6pm UK), which is what I've set as the default (`release_time_utc` in fiction.json - one line to change). Reasoning: RR's readership is majority North American with a strong EU minority; 17:00 UTC catches EU readers in their evening and US readers at lunch, and your chapter then has the whole US evening peak (when Rising Stars/trending browsing is heaviest) to accumulate views and followers while still fresh. Earlier (e.g. midnight UTC) wastes the US evening; consistency matters more than the exact slot, so pick once and hold it.</p>
<p>**Sequence when the language police finishes**: apply confirmed fixes → re-run `update-submission` so the prologue is the fixed text → you press Launch → `go --yes`. The workflow's still running; I'll report the haul when it lands.</p>
</div>

2. Key Technical Concepts:<br>

Edit form: /author-dashboard/chapters/edit/&lt;chapterId&gt; with Status="Published", Id, DontNotifyReaders="true".<br>
   - Submission vs fiction id: submission 222993; Launch button (/author-dashboard/submissions/release/222993) created live fiction **189685** at /fiction/189685/how-to-save-the-world.<br>

   - Commenter comments JSON: comments/&lt;rel&gt;.comments.json {file, comments:[{id, created, body, resolved, anchor?:{quote,prefix,suffix,...}}]}; lp-prefixed ids are Claude-filed borderline comments; anchor-survival triage distinguishes actioned vs live comments (short quotes match spuriously).<br>

3. Files and Code Sections:<br>

Posting payload (final version):<br>
       ```python<br>
       data = {"__RequestVerificationToken": token, "Status": "New", "fid": str(fid),<br>
               "EditorUsed": "TinyMce", "Title": f"Chapter {n} - {title}",<br>
               "Content": body, "PreAuthorNotes": top, "PostAuthorNotes": note,<br>
               "DeleteDraft": "false"}<br>
       if e["mode"] == "publish": data["action"] = "publish"<br>
       else:<br>
           data["action"] = "draft"<br>
           stamp = when.strftime("%Y-%m-%d %H:%M")<br>
           data["ScheduledRelease"] = stamp<br>
           data["AdjustedScheduledRelease"] = stamp<br>
       ```<br>
     - `cmd_fix_ch1(yes)` — re-POSTs chapter 3892228's edit form: Title "Chapter 1 - Pellings, half past twelve, soft", Content=body only, note moved to PostAuthorNotes, DontNotifyReaders=true.<br>
     - Dispatch: plan / go [--yes] [--only N] / fix-ch1 [--yes] / update-submission / create-fiction / login / whoami / probe.<br>
   - `tools/rr_convert.py` — System boxes changed from styled div to RR-style blue table:<br>
     ```python<br>
     TABLE_STYLE = ("margin: 1em auto; width: 90%; max-width: 32em; "<br>
         "background: #004b7a; border: 2px solid #7fb3d1; border-collapse: separate; "<br>
         "border-radius: 6px;")<br>
     CELL_STYLE = "padding: 0.8em 1.2em; text-align: center; color: #ffffff;"<br>
     # flush_box emits &lt;table style=...&gt;&lt;tbody&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td style=...&gt;lines&lt;br&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;/tbody&gt;&lt;/table&gt;<br>
     ```<br>

<p>So: act one runs mostly 450-900 words per chapter, act two 1,200-1,600. RR's comfortable norm is roughly 1,500-2,500 words, and readers do complain about sub-1,000-word chapters - your instinct matches the data, especially for chapters 6-11 and 16/19/22, which are a few minutes' reading each.</p>
<p>Worth saying: this is actually the plan we deviated from. `notes/process.md` always said scenes are the drafting unit and **chapters (~2,000 words) get assembled from finished scenes afterwards**, possibly cutting mid-scene - and then I uploaded one scene per chapter. Grouping 2 scenes (act two) to 3 scenes (the short act-one runs) gets you ~1,800-2,400 per chapter, ~18-20 chapters total instead of 40, which is right in RR's sweet spot.</p>
<p>The catch is publication state: 11 are live (readers may have started) and 29 are scheduled drafts. Options, roughly:</p>
<p>1. **Restructure everything now** - delete the 29 drafts, merge the 11 live ones into ~4-5 chapters (RR lets you edit/delete published chapters), re-upload merged. Cleanest long-term; slightly disruptive if anyone's mid-read, but it's day 0 with presumably tiny readership, so this is the cheapest it will ever be.<br>
2. **Keep 1-11 as-is, merge only 12-40** - no reader disruption, but the size jump mid-book looks odd and the thin early chapters are exactly where new readers judge you.<br>
3. **Leave it** - not recommended given your read.</p>
<p>I'd do option 1, today, before the Sep 1 release fires. Want me to draft a proposed chapter grouping (scene → chapter mapping with titles) for you to approve before touching RR?</p>
